A first rule, the last free cell along the line of sight, was dropped in the implementation: in the default view it leaves an empty Hull A rack sideways, the outer row on the plate couldn't be reached and the ghost appeared cells away from the cursor. **Validity is Core's:** `LoadPlanner.MoveCrates(plan, ship, moves)` with `CrateMove(Crate, RackId, Position)` is **atomic** – every crate leaves its cells, then all take the new ones, then the rules of `CrateLayout.Problems` run over the result (so crates of one move may carry each other); `CrateLayout.NewProblem` reports only what the move breaks (a problem of a file edited by hand stays out), and `LoadPlanner.MoveProblem` returns that text for the racks touched. The preview asks the same function the drop does (`CrateDrop` in the view): a **ghost** at the target, cyan where it fits, red where it doesn't with the reason as tool tip; releasing on red moves nothing and the notice line says why (D24). Nothing slides down. `MoveCrate` is `MoveCrates` with one move; `MovePlacement` and the overlay's placement drag are gone (this answers D22's open question). **Legend:** right drag of a stop line into the view runs `MoveStop` on the rack under the cursor (re-layout in the plan's fill order); the stop's crates fade, the target rack gets a cyan edge, refusal goes to the notice line; a stop already wholly in that rack stays as it is. Left on the line stays the tick (D16); delivered crates can't be dragged but carry and are drop targets (D24). **Selection** (T3.14b): right click on a crate toggles it, on free or empty space clears; a drag from a selected crate moves the whole selection with every crate keeping its offset in ship coordinates (origin + position, D14) to the anchor; a crate landing in no rack makes the whole move invalid. The selection is view state, not saved, and goes with every plan change and with the lock: `CrateSelection` is bound to the scene it was made in, and every plan change builds a new scene, so no crate of an old plan reaches Core. A right click on a delivered crate keeps the selection. Selected crates get a magenta edge (`MissionPalette.Selection`), the fill stays the stop colour; while dragging, every crate of the selection shows its ghost, and a selection reaching past every rack shows the grabbed crate's ghost red with "A selected crate would land outside the racks." (`CrateMove.Shift` returns null, `CrateDrop.OutsideRacks`). Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval.
- **Counter-argument:** The column rule is gravity at aiming time, which is what "nothing slides down" was meant to rule out for moves; a floating target can no longer be picked on purpose for a 1 SCU crate, only a larger crate can still hang over an edge. Refusal texts are now the findings' wording ("Crates of Orison and Riker overlap in rack 2"), less specific about which crate moved than the old `MoveCrate` sentences. The ship scene now carries the plan and the ship so the view can ask Core while dragging; a delegate bound from the view model would have kept the scene a pure drawing. The min corner, not the grabbed cell, goes to the target: grabbing the far end of a 4 SCU crate and dropping it puts its near end there.
- **Consequence:** At the lift the overlay follows the plate crate by crate; a whole stop changes racks from the legend. Undo is dragging back. Not part of this: a selection rectangle, keyboard modifiers, undo, turning crates, sliding crates down, middle drag, dragging between overlays.
- **Addendum T3.13c:** `CrateMove.Shift` keeps the ship offset only for a selection over several racks. A selection from one rack keeps its grid offsets to the anchor, so a stack stays a stack between racks stacking in opposite directions (port +y, starboard −y, D26). Over several racks each crate's box moves by the anchor's box offset and lands in the first rack, in rack order, whose grid holds its min corner (`Rack.ToGrid`), otherwise the move is invalid as before. Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval.
- **Addendum T3.15:** The selection is no longer the view's alone: `ShipViewControl.Selection` is a two-way dependency property bound to `OverlayViewModel.Selection`, so the header's split and merge buttons (D27) can read it and select the crates they made. It is still not saved and still bound to its scene. Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval.
- **Addendum (B3, D65):** The preview shows Core's outcome, not a problem: cyan ghosts for every crate that changes place (dragged, laid anew, displaced), the originals faded, red ghosts at the cells aimed at only for a refusal, the notices or the refusal as tool tip. "Nothing slides down", the column rule as the only gravity and `CrateDrop.OutsideRacks` are gone: a selection reaching past every rack is laid into the anchor's rack. `LoadPlanner.MoveProblem` is replaced by `ResolveMove`/`ResolveStopMove`; preview and drop still call one function, and the preview asks again only when target rack, cell, the crate held or the landing cells change (E2). The legend drag gets the same ghosts next to its cyan rack edge. Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval.
- **Addendum (B6, D69):** The legend drag moves a line's crates (one mission, one destination) or, from a header, a mission's, no longer a whole stop: `Gesture.MoveLegend` with the crates, `LoadPlanner.ResolveMoveToRack`; `MoveStop`/`ResolveStopMove` are gone. A legend check box makes the same selection as right clicks. Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval.
- **Addendum (K3.2, D71):** turning a crate is part of it now: the wheel during a single-crate drag. A selection keeps how its crates lie.
- **Addendum (K4.2, D29):** the magenta selection edge, the red problem edge and the cyan and red ghost edges on crates get a dark under-stroke (`MissionPalette.EdgeUnderStroke`, black at alpha `A0`, 4.5 px under the 2.5 px edge, round joins; proposals for Martin's look gate), because Tol's purples come closer to the magenta than any Okabe-Ito colour (ΔE76 59.4 and 68.3 against 78.0). It applies in every palette, the default included; rack edges (target, violated rack) keep their single line. Measured offscreen on the Hull A with eight stops: without a selection the view is pixel-identical to before, with one the 4,420 differing pixels all lie on the selected crates. Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval. **Counter-argument:** the edge looks heavier than before in every palette, also where it read well on its own; a thin dark seam now runs round a selected crate even on a dark scene, where it adds nothing.
- **Addendum (K4.4): the selection edge is a setting.** Martin at K4.2's look gate: colour, width and contour of the selected crates' edge in the preferences. `appearance.crates.selectionColor` (default magenta `#FFFF00FF`, any alpha), `selectionWidth` (2.5 px, 1–8: a hairline up to a band that still leaves a 1 SCU face visible at the default zoom) and `selectionOutline`, the contour, shaped like a halo (`enabled`, `color`, `size`; on, `#A0000000`, 1), its size the width on each side of the edge, so the default is K4.2's 2.5 px over 4.5 px; optional, no schema bump (D31), in the Crates section with its reset and in every look file (D77). The theme builds two frozen pens (`CratesSelectionPen`, `CratesSelectionOutlinePen`, the second removed when off, like a halo), which the ship view binds; `MissionPalette.Selection` is gone, the default lives in the settings model. Problem and ghost edges keep their fixed under-stroke: they are states, not a choice. Default drawing unchanged by construction (same pens in the same order). Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval. **Counter-argument:** a selection colour can now be a stop colour, or the problem red, and nothing warns; the reset is the way back. A contour of size 20 on an 8 px edge is a 48 px band that covers neighbouring crates. Inside `selectionOutline` missing keys take the generic halo defaults (D31), so `{ "size": 2 }` is off.
- **Addendum (K6.2): the selection survives the lock.** At the elevator the lock is the normal state, and the selection (crates marked, legend boxes ticked) is what the player needs there; until now the lock cleared it ("locked at the lift means loading, not planning: a selection left over must not move with the next drag"). The lock no longer touches it: edges, dimming, the legend's boxes and the info line stay while locked, and unlocking finds them as they were. The only reason given in the code was the next drag, not a running one: a drag running when the lock comes still ends without a move, through the released capture (`OnLostMouseCapture`). A drag that never got the capture had no such end and could have run on a later button up after unlocking; the lock now ends it as well (`ShipViewControl.IsDragging`, tested without a window, where no capture exists). The selection still goes with every plan change (a sync, a tick, an undo): unchanged, a point of its own if the elevator needs it. Measured offscreen (real overlay, Martin's Hurston plan): 9 crates selected through a line box and a header box stay selected, with the same boxes ticked, locked and unlocked again. Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval.
  - **Counter-argument:** The old reason holds: after unlocking, a right drag of a selected crate takes the whole selection along, which the player may have forgotten while loading; the selection is visible and every move can be undone (K2.4). The auto-lock (T3.3) switches with the game's cursor, so the selection now outlives every cursor change in the game too. A selection kept while locked is lost anyway at the next sync or tick, which at the elevator happen often.
- **Addendum (T8.4, D83): the drop target on the line of sight.** `DropTarget.Resolve` takes the ray from the camera on screen (`CameraMath.Unproject`) in the rack the hit test names (the first on the line, D21); the rules stay the data's: a crate on the line takes the drop on top, none if its stack is full; else the frontmost free cell, and the drop falls down its column along the rack's stack axis. The line picks the cell the cursor shows, the grid does the fall, so it holds for any line, also one running against the stack axis (a rack under the hull seen from below, tested on a built rack; no ship file has one). Neither the plate's plane (the cursor over a column's top would aim at the plate behind it) nor the rack's box alone (no cell under free outlines) would keep "the cell is what the cursor shows". Cells behind the camera don't count, the one around it from the camera on (`RayBox.Ahead`); the orthographic line had no "behind". A release now ends its drag itself: before, only `LostMouseCapture` did, so a drag without the capture stayed and swallowed every later press (also in `main`; with the capture nothing changes). Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval.

## D26 – Stack axis is rack data

- **Context:** D14 fixed the grid to the ship axes (length → x, width → y, stack → z) as a drawing convention and left the physical stacking direction to the backlog (`orientation`). In the game it differs per rack (Martin): the Hull A's spindle plates stack from the spindle outwards, interior floors stack up, hull undersides stack down; crates always lie flat against their plate (D9). The overlay drew the Hull A's crates on top of each other where the game puts them side by side, so the in-game check open since T3.10 (2 × 8 SCU in Rack 1) could not pass.
- **Decision:** Ship file `schemaVersion` 4. Each rack has a required `stackAxis`: `"+x"`, `"-x"`, `"+y"`, `"-y"`, `"+z"` or `"-z"`, the ship direction away from the plate. One axis, no orientation triple: length lies along x (on z when the stack is ±x), width takes the remaining axis; a negative axis counts from the rack's max side, so `origin` stays the min corner. Hull A: Rack 1/2 `+y`, Rack 3/4 `-y`, same bounds as before. The grid stays abstract: `CrateLayout`, the support rule (D24, along stack), `Problems` and moves work in grid coordinates; the **only** grid-to-ship mapping is `Rack` in Core (`Bounds`, `CellBox(position, size)`, its inverse `ToGrid(box)`), used by the view, the ghost, the drop target and `CrateMove.Shift`. Width rows fill in grid order: the old "centreline first" rule read `Origin.Y`, which with width on z measures nothing; the port/starboard mirror now comes from the stack axis. `Shift` keeps grid offsets within one rack (D25 addendum). Racks built in code default to `+z`, the old convention. Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval.
- **Counter-argument:** One axis can't describe a rack turned in its plane or tilted; an orientation triple could, at the price of a format nobody fills in correctly by eye. Grid order instead of "centreline first" is a new drawing rule, and starboard layouts of new plans differ from older ones. `Shift` now has two semantics, chosen by whether the selection spans racks. A rack built in code without an axis silently stacks up.
- **Consequence:** v3 ship files are rejected with "schemaVersion 3 is not supported (expected 4)"; every new ship names its axis per rack (docs/ship-format.md, read it from Erec's guide or in the hangar). Saved plans need no change: positions are grid positions. On a rack stacking along ±x, length runs along z and "bow first" fills from the top. Replaces D14's sentence "Grid axes are fixed to the ship axes" and D21's centreline rule; free rotation per rack stays backlog until a ship needs it.

## D27 – Split and merge within the footprint

- **Context:** The kiosk sometimes delivers other crates than the derived mix (D23): two 2 SCU crates instead of one 4. The plan then shows crates the plate doesn't have, and the player can't follow it. D24 removed D22's split and merge of placements; with crates as the unit they come back as operations on single crates, under the game's rules: fixed sizes only (1/2/4/8/16/24/32), one commodity and one destination, a merge only when the sum is a crate size again.
- **Decision:** `LoadPlanner.SplitCrate(saved, ship, crate[, parts])` and `MergeCrates(saved, ship, crates)` work on the `SavedPlan`, so plan and missions change together. **Footprint rule:** a split lays its parts with `CrateLayout.PlaceWithin`, the layout rule restricted to the old crate's cells, largest first; a merge needs the crates' cells to be exactly the box of the new size in its only orientation (`CrateLayout.BlockProblem`: overlap, "not adjacent", "don't form a block", "would be a 4×1×1 crate, 4 SCU crates are 2×2×1") and puts the crate at their min corner. Either way no cell changes owner, so nothing above or beside loses support and no new finding is possible; the hints are derived anew and stay. **Default split without a dialog:** the two largest parts that add up and fit (`CrateMix.DefaultSplit`: 2 → 1+1, 4 → 2+2, 8 → 4+4, 16 → 8+8, 24 → 16+8, 32 → 16+16); finer is splitting again, a 1 SCU crate can't be split, `parts` stays as API for a later editor. No menus on the overlay, and two clicks at the lift beat a dialog. **Merge rules:** one destination, commodity and rack, not delivered; the sum a crate size and not above the contract's max crate size (the kiosk never delivers larger, D23); all crates from one contract item, because an item's SCU is contract data and must not move between items. **Missions follow:** the contract item that holds the crates (the first in mission order) takes the new crate list as an override, largest first (`MissionItem.WithCrateList`); equal to the derived mix it is dropped (D23 addendum). The `SavedPlan` constructor checks the crate multiset as before; a failure there is a bug, not a player error. **Order in the plan:** the parts take the split crate's index, the merged crate the first merged index, so legend and placements keep their order and split then merge gives the original crates. **Errors like D24:** caller errors stay `ArgumentException` (another ship, a crate not in the plan or named twice, delivered crates, fewer than two crates to merge); refusals are a `PlanException` with the reason. `SplitProblem` and `MergeProblem` return that reason or null: one check for the button and the operation (D25). **Overlay:** two glyph buttons in the header left of the pencil, Split (Segoe MDL2 Cut) and Merge (Link), not focusable, hidden while locked. Enabled by the selection: split for exactly one crate Core can split, merge for two or more Core can merge; disabled they fade, and the tool tip says why (`ToolTipService.ShowOnDisabled`). After the operation the new crates stay selected, so "split, split again" and "merge back" need no new pick. A refusal goes to the notice line ("Split refused: …"). Saved through `ShowPlan` and `PlanSaver` like every change. Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval.
- **Counter-argument:** The default split can differ from the kiosk: 8 → 4+2+2 takes two steps (8 → 4+4, then 4 → 2+2). A merge needs the crates side by side in the right shape, so sometimes a drag comes first. Crates from two equal contract rows can't be merged into one. A typed override in an unsorted order comes back sorted after a split or merge. An editor left open doesn't see a split or merge until it is opened again. The selection is no longer pure view state (D25 addendum). Two more buttons narrow the header: at the minimum width the ship name is cut short.
- **Consequence:** At the lift the plan follows the kiosk's crates in a few clicks, and the editor shows the overridden item in italics. Not part of this: a split dialog with a choice of parts, merging across racks, merging with moving (not adjacent → drag first), detecting the kiosk's mix, undo.
- **Addendum (T7.1c):** with the 32 as 8×2×2 (D57) a 24 + 8 fits its footprint too, so "the two largest sizes" would now give 24 + 8. The default stays 16 + 16; the rule is now: halve the crate if half is a crate size, else the largest size that fits plus the rest (24 → 16 + 8). It gives the same table as before, so nothing changes for haulers. With every crate from 8 SCU up n × 2 × 2 cells, every split of a crate fits its footprint (a 32 has 211); the footprint check in `SplitProblem` stays for shapes to come. Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval.
- **Addendum (T7.4d):** A merge needs crates of one item (`Crate.Item`, D68), also without pickup; the item rebooked is the crates' own, no longer the first one holding them. Split parts keep their item. Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval.

## D28 – Blur means a text halo, not a backdrop blur

- **Context:** Phase 4 makes the overlay's look configurable. "Blur" on the wish list can mean blurring what lies behind the window (frosted glass) or a soft outline around text and glyphs. The overlay stands over very different scenes, bright at Orison, dark at Lorville, and right next to the cargo lift and the kiosk the player has to see.
- **Decision:** Blur is a **halo around text and glyphs** (legend, rack numbers, header and tabs, buttons), its colour and strength set per section. No backdrop blur behind the window: it would blur the lift and the kiosk under the overlay, the very things the player is looking at. Readability over bright and dark scenes comes from the halo, background transparency and frames. Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval.
- **Counter-argument:** A frosted backdrop reads well over any scene without tuning each section. A halo per text element may cost render time in the software-rendered layered window (D2), one effect per element instead of one per window. A halo doesn't help a crate fill over a background of the same hue.
- **Consequence:** Backdrop blur stays in the backlog as a spike (DWM backdrop vs. `AllowsTransparency`; whether the two work together is open). The outlined rack numbers (D15) become one case of the halo.

## D29 – Stop colours stay fixed (Okabe-Ito)

- **Context:** Phase 4 lets players tune colours, fonts and transparency. The stop colours (D15: Okabe-Ito without black, plus white) are what the player matches between the legend and the plate.
- **Decision:** Stop colours are **not configurable**. Okabe-Ito was chosen to stay distinguishable with the common colour vision deficiencies; a free palette, or a shared theme, could lose that without anyone noticing. Readability over bright or dark scenes is tuned with frames, transparency and halo (D28), not with the hues. Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval.
- **Counter-argument:** Some players want stronger or different colours, and a player with a rarer deficiency may be better served by another palette than Okabe-Ito. A fixed colour over a scene of the same hue (orange over Lorville) can still read badly.
- **Consequence:** `MissionPalette` stays as it is. Alternative palettes are backlog, and only ones checked for colour vision deficiency.
- **Addendum (K4.2): a choice of checked palettes.** The backlog item is done in its narrow form: `appearance.crates.stopPalette` (optional, no schema bump, D31) picks one of three palettes, each documented as safe for deuteranopia and protanopia by its source; no free colours. **Okabe-Ito** (8, the default and the colours of before: Okabe & Ito 2008, values as in Wong 2011, without black, with white, D15), **Tol Bright** (6) and **Tol Muted** (7) from Paul Tol's "Colour schemes" (sronpersonalpages.nl/~pault, read 25 Oct 2026). One rule for what leaves a source: a colour that vanishes on the dark overlay or looks like a state colour is no stop colour. So Tol Bright loses its grey `#BBBBBB` (Tol's own bad-data colour; in the overlay grey means delivered, Martin at the approval), Tol Muted its indigo `#332288` and wine `#882255` (1.5:1 and 2.1:1 against the window's `#141414`, the darkest Okabe-Ito colour has 3.6:1). The IBM palette of the brief is out: IBM publishes no hex values of its own, only secondary sources do, and its orange `#FE6100` lies nearer the problem red (ΔE76 26) than any colour before. **Measured** (CIE76 in CIELAB from sRGB): nearest to the problem red `#FF2A2A` Okabe vermillion 33.6, Tol Bright red 43.1, Tol Muted rose 55.7; nearest to the delivered grey `#5A5A5A` Okabe blue 42.2, Tol Bright blue 34.0, Tol Muted teal 42.1 (white 61.8, Tol's sand 62.4: ΔE76 weighs lightness fully, and delivered crates are dark); nearest to the selection magenta 78.0, 68.3, 59.4; to the drag cyan 42.9, 33.2, 34.1. A test holds every colour of every palette at ΔE76 ≥ 33.6, today's minimum, against the problem red and the delivered grey (Martin: nearness, not equality, so it also catches the next palette); magenta and cyan are edges on top and get a dark under-stroke instead (D25 addendum). **More stops than colours:** a stop keeps its ordinal in D64's order (`MissionPalette.Assign` no longer wraps), the palette turns it into colour (ordinal mod count) and hatch per round: plain, then stripes "/", then "\"; after three rounds both repeat and the legend's line says so ("More than 8 stops: colors repeat, hatched from stop 9."). Stripes over dots: lines are what the software-rendered layered window (D2) draws cheapest, and two directions read apart at swatch size. The hatch is a frozen tiled `DrawingBrush` in absolute units (6 DIP tile, 1.5 DIP stripes, proposals for the look gate), fixed to the screen like every pen here (D32), so it keeps its size at every zoom and runs on across faces; stripes are black or white, whichever has the higher WCAG contrast to the fill. `StopBrushes` is the one place an ordinal becomes a brush, for crates and swatches alike, so a hatch means the same crate in view and legend; the tool tip names the destination as before. Delivered crates stay grey and plain, the selection keeps its magenta, problems their red. The palette is `OverlayViewModel.StopPalette`, bound by the ship view and the legend, not part of the scene: a new scene empties the selection (D25), a new palette only repaints. **Measured** (`StopHatchTimingTests`, Release, median of 30, three runs): C2 with 16 stops and 64 crates at 800 × 600, 16 plain colours 24.7–25.9 ms, Okabe-Ito with 8 hatched stops 25.8–26.3 ms. Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval.
  - **Counter-argument:** Three palettes don't serve a rarer deficiency (tritanopia) any better than one, and a player can't fix a colour that reads badly over one scene; that stays the frame's and the halo's job (D28). Dropping colours from a source makes it no longer quite the source's palette, even if the rule is one. ΔE76 is a coarse measure (it overstates distances in saturated blues and ignores how small the crate face is), and a bound equal to today's minimum is knife-edge: Tol Bright's blue passes the grey at 34.0. A hatched crate is busier at small sizes, and at 6 DIP tiles its stripes may shimmer while the ship turns. Swatches are 12 px, two stripes wide.

## D30 – Settings apply on change, reset per section

- **Context:** The settings window (T4.2) needs a commit model: OK/Cancel as in classic dialogs, or every change taking effect at once. Window position and size already save on change (D13, T3.4).
- **Decision:** Every change **applies to the overlay at once** and is saved like the window bounds (debounced, atomic, D13); no OK, Cancel or Apply. Each section has "Reset to defaults". There is no intermediate state between what the settings window shows and what the overlay uses, and the player tunes readability while looking at the game. Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval.
- **Counter-argument:** Without Cancel, a failed experiment is undone by hand or by resetting the whole section; a dialog with Cancel would keep a snapshot. Dragging a slider changes the overlay many times per second; the debounce limits the writes, not the redraws.
- **Consequence:** No snapshot, no undo in the settings window; the per-section reset is the way back. Hotkeys (T4.4) follow the same rule: a new combination is registered at once.

## D31 – Settings schema 2: appearance as data, theme as DynamicResources

- **Context:** Phase 4 makes the overlay's look configurable (D28–D30). Each value needs a place in settings.json and a way into the views that the settings window (T4.2) can change while the overlay is open. Without a file the overlay must look exactly as before.
- **Decision:** settings.json **schema 2** adds `appearance` with `window`, `header`, `legend`, `crates`, `buttons` (docs/settings-format.md); `overlay` and `hotkeys` are unchanged, legend visibility and position come with T4.3 as an optional field. Schema 1 is a subset: it loads as is with the default look and is written as 2 on the next save, not on loading (D13: the file stays as the player left it). The defaults are the old literals, guarded by a test; the legend's font size defaults to `null` = the Windows message font size, because that is what the legend used (11 on the development machine, not 12). **Transparency is the alpha of a colour** (`#AARRGGBB`), no separate opacity; the crates' colours are fixed (D29), so their fill and edge alpha are numbers 0–255. Unknown keys and wrong types keep the strict rule (whole file → defaults + notice, D13); **a number out of range is clamped, a colour that isn't one takes its default**, both with a notice line, and the rest applies (`AppearanceRules`, WPF-free). Backgrounds that take clicks keep alpha ≥ 1 (a layered window passes clicks on alpha 0). **Halo** per section: `enabled`, `color`, `size`; text gets a frozen, shared `DropShadowEffect` without offset (soft, D28), the rack numbers keep their sharp outline with the halo's colour and width. The button frame is the glyph buttons'; "Open editor" keeps its outline in the legend colour. **Theme:** `ThemeResources.Apply` fills `Application.Resources` from the settings at startup, keys `<Section><Part><Kind>` (`ThemeResources.Keys`); the views bind with `DynamicResource`, the ship view through dependency properties with `AffectsRender`. A halo that is off removes its key: assigning null through the indexer makes a DynamicResource fall back to the property's default instead of delivering null (spike), and every property bound to such a key defaults to null. The model is positional records with default parameters: in .NET 10 the source generator sets omitted init properties to null/0 instead of their initializers (spike; fixed in .NET 11 per the docs). A missing or null section or halo means its defaults. Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval.
- **Counter-argument:** Alpha in hex (`A0` = 63 %) is hard to edit by hand; a percentage field would read better. One typo in a key name still resets everything, hotkeys included once the overlay moves. `size` means blur radius for text and stroke width for rack numbers. Defaults live in code, not in App.xaml, so the XAML designer shows no theme colours. Inside a halo object missing keys take the generic halo defaults, so a crates halo written as `{ "size": 5 }` is off. A `DropShadowEffect` per text element costs render time in the software-rendered layered window (D2); default off.
- **Consequence:** T4.2 calls `Apply` again on every change and resets a section to its `Default`. `MissionPalette` keeps only fixed colours; the legend swatches keep their own alpha. The rack number font size comes with T4.6 as `crates.rackNumberFontSize`, legend visibility and position with T4.3, both without a schema bump. An older app can't read a schema 2 file.

## D32 – Rack numbers in screen size

- **Context:** The rack numbers grew with the zoom and the window: `max(12, 0.9 × scale)`, scale = fit × zoom. A font size setting (`crates.rackNumberFontSize`, D31) would have meant a different size at every zoom, and the number "breathed" while orbiting, because the fit follows the camera (D15). D21's backlog said numbers and lines grow; the lines didn't: `ViewTransform` maps every point before drawing, no `RenderTransform`, so pens were in screen pixels already (frame and rack edges 1, free cells and crate edges 0.5, red, cyan and selection 2.5, the number outline the halo size).
- **Decision:** The number has a fixed screen size, `crates.rackNumberFontSize`, optional, no schema bump, default 27 (the old size at zoom 1 in the default overlay with the sample plan, guarded by a test), range 8–48 of its own (the legend's 8–32 would leave the default near the top). Where the number would be taller than the shortest rack side on screen (`RackNumberSize`: shortest edge of all racks in cells × scale; Hull A 2) it shrinks with the racks, down to 12 as before; below that numbers overlap. A setting below 12 is kept. Line widths are unchanged. Hit test and drop target are untouched: the size only enters `OnRender`. Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval.
- **Counter-argument:** The setting is a maximum, not a fixed size: in a small overlay at 0.5× the numbers still shrink. The rule is a heuristic, how tall a rack side appears depends on the camera, and in the default overlay at 0.5× the margin is small (cap 30 against 27). Hiding numbers below a threshold would be simpler to read but would take the rack number away in the small overview, where the player needs it at the spindle (D9); letting them overlap at any size would make neighbouring numbers cover each other.
- **Consequence:** The number no longer changes while orbiting at normal sizes. The settings window (T4.2) gets the font size in its Crates section. A ship with thin racks (one cell) gets smaller numbers sooner.
- **Addendum T7.3:** The minimum of 12 now also bounds the bow label (the floor's room shrinks it no further) and the free-cell grid (drawn in full only from 12 px per cell), see D61. The number's size rule itself is unchanged; on a long ship the number stands above its rack.
- **Addendum (T8.2, D83):** In the perspective view the scale is the pixels per cell in the plane through the target square to the view (`CameraMath`); `RackNumberSize.For` and its 12 to `crates.rackNumberFontSize` are unchanged, so every number has the same size, near racks and far ones alike. The test that derives the default 27 still reckons with the orthographic fit; T8.6 justifies it anew or drops it.

## D33 – Legend position and visibility

- **Context:** The legend stood below the ship view across the full width, its lines left-aligned. Next to the kiosk and depending on the overlay's shape it fits better on top or below, left, centred or right; a player who knows the colours wants the stop lines gone. The settings window (T4.2) will switch both while the overlay is open.
- **Decision:** settings.json gets a top-level `legend` with `visible` (default `true`) and `position` (default `"bottom-left"`), optional, no schema bump (D31); top level because it is layout, not look, and "Reset to defaults" per section (D30) must not move the legend with its colours. `position` is one of six names (`top|bottom`-`left|center|right`, lower case), stored as a string and checked by `AppearanceRules` like a colour: an unknown name takes `bottom-left` with a short notice ("edge-alignment, e.g. bottom-right"; the six names would be three amber lines in the default overlay), instead of an enum converter that would make the whole file invalid (D13); wrong types and unknown keys stay strict. `LegendPosition` (edge + alignment) parses it. **Left/centre/right moves a block as wide as its lines**, inside left-aligned so the swatches stand in one column; the hidden hint reserve (D16) counts towards its width and height, so ticking moves neither the block nor the ship view in any position. **Top** is header → notices → block → view; notices always run full width at the outer edge (very bottom, or right under the header), outside the block, so a notice coming and going doesn't push the block sideways. `visible: false` collapses only the stop lines; empty state, violations, hints with their reserve, the palette line and notices stay. Dock, alignment and visibility are style triggers bound to `OverlayViewModel.IsLegendVisible` and `LegendPosition`, not DynamicResources: structure, not theme values, testable without a window; changing `DockPanel.Dock` re-measures the parent (`OnDockChanged`). The legend drag (D25) needs no change: the view captures the mouse and works in its own coordinates, above or below it alike. Checked offscreen with `RenderTargetBitmap`: bottom-left is pixel-identical to before, and ticking every stop moves the view in none of the twelve layouts, switched live on one window. Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval.
- **Counter-argument:** Two `legend` keys on two levels invite `appearance.legend.position`, an unknown key that resets the whole file. The six names live in our own code instead of the serializer. A block as wide as its lines has a ragged right edge at `right`, and a violation after a move changes its width, so at centre or right it shifts sideways (editing, not ticking, accepted like D20). Notices stay left-aligned when the legend stands right. On top, a notice that appears ("Move refused") pushes the ship view down while the player looks at the crate just dropped; below, the view shrinks too but its top edge stays. A fixed one-line notice reserve like the hints would fix that, only once it bothers in the game.
- **Consequence:** With `visible: false` dragging a whole stop from the legend (D25) is gone with the lines; a stop is then moved through the crate selection (T3.14b), and settings-format.md says so next to "hides only the stop lines". T4.2 sets the two view model properties besides calling `ThemeResources.Apply`. Unchanged and the same in every position: the view clips its drawing (`ClipToBounds`) but the hit test doesn't, so a drop over the legend can hit a rack part zoomed out of sight (backlog).
- **Addendum (T5.1), notices fixed at the bottom left:** Notices no longer follow `legend.position`. They are a child of the overlay's outer DockPanel of their own, docked at the bottom before the legend area, so they are always the bottom line, full width and left-aligned; a bottom legend stands above them, a top legend under the header. Nothing gives way sideways: at `bottom-*` the layout is the same as before, at `top-*` a notice coming and going no longer pushes the ship view down while the player looks at the crate just dropped, the view's top edge stays. Reason: the ContractTracker import (T5.3–T5.5) brings more notices. Replaces "notices … right under the header" above. Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval. **Counter-argument:** At `top-*` a notice stands far from the legend it used to belong to, and the ship view still shrinks when one appears, now from below; a fixed one-line reserve would stop that too, still only once it bothers in the game. Overlaying the notices on the ship view's bottom-left corner would move nothing, but could cover crates and rack numbers.
- **Addendum (T7.4d):** With pickups in the plan the stop lines get headers ("From A, B", D68) inside the same list, so `visible: false` hides them with the lines. Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval.
- **Addendum (K1.4):** The backlog item in the consequence above is done: the hit test now takes the view's area, like the drawing. `SceneHitTest.HitAt`, `DestinationAt` and `ToolTipAt` have overloads with the transform and the view's size that hit nothing outside the view's rectangle (edges included), and the view uses only those: hover, right-click selection, drop target, legend drag, double-click. A crate or a legend line released over the legend (or the header) resolves to no gesture, so nothing moves; the preview shows no ghost and no target. Measured before: with Rack 1 zoomed below a 400 × 300 view, the point (200, 350) over the legend hit `rack-0` and a legend line released there was a move; after, no hit, no move. Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval. **Counter-argument:** a drag that ends a pixel past the view's edge is now cancelled where before it might have landed on a rack drawn right at the edge; the player has to zoom or pan the rack into view first.
- **Addendum (T8.3, D83):** the overloads take the camera (`CameraMath`) instead of the transform and the view's size; the rectangle is the camera's viewport, in DIPs like the mouse position. The rule and the measured case are unchanged (`SceneHitTestTests`, now on rays).

## D34 – Settings window: one settings session, system look, gear next to the padlock

- **Context:** Editing settings.json by hand puts haulers off (D17), and readability is tuned in the game, not in a text editor (D30). T4.1–T4.6 built the model, its checks and the DynamicResources. `OverlayBoundsSaver` kept its own copy of `AppSettings`: a look saved from a settings window would have been overwritten with the old look by the next move of the overlay.
- **Decision:** **`SettingsSession` is the one owner of the current `AppSettings`.** Its constructor applies the loaded settings at startup without writing (D31); `ChangeLook` from the settings window runs the same `AppearanceRules.Normalize` as loading, applies through the same method (`ThemeResources.Apply` plus the view model's legend layout) and saves debounced by 500 ms; `ChangeBounds` from `OverlayBoundsSaver` (still debounced there) saves at once, a pending look with it; `Flush` on the overlay's `Closed` writes what is pending. A correction from the window keeps its notice after saving, so a slider with a wrong limit shows at once instead of after a restart. **`SettingsViewModel`** is WPF-free and returns appearance and legend layout, not a whole `AppSettings`: its copy would hold stale bounds. Building blocks `ColourField` (hex `#RRGGBB`, `#` optional, plus an alpha slider 0–255 with a percent label and a swatch on a checkerboard; eight digits are invalid so field and slider can't disagree; an invalid hex tints the field, applies nothing and shows no notice) and `HaloEditor` (switch, colour, size), reused by T4.2b. Slider limits are `AppearanceRules`' constants via `x:Static`; clickable backgrounds start at alpha 1. "Use Windows text size" is `fontSize: null`; unticked, the slider starts at the Windows size clamped into 8–32. Reset per section to its `Default`, applied once; legend layout is a section of its own (D33). **The window has the system look**, not the theme, so nobody themes away the tool to undo the theme. Like the editor (D19): one instance, owned by the overlay, topmost, `ShowActivated=False`, hidden with the overlay and while auto-locked (`OverlayViewModel.AreToolWindowsHidden`, shared with the editor through `OverlayWindow.HideWithOverlay`); Esc and X close it; it opens next to the overlay (`SettingsWindowPlacement`), the position is not saved. **The gear sits right of the pencil, next to the padlock:** window controls right, plan controls left; hidden while locked like every header button. **Way out of an invisible theme:** the gear keeps alpha ≥ 1 and takes the hover, its system tool tip "Settings" shows over an invisible glyph; the README says where to hover, or to delete `appearance` from settings.json. No start switch, no contrast floor. Hotkeys get their section with T4.4. Split into T4.2a (session, gear, window, sections Window, Legend, Legend layout) and T4.2b (Header, Crates, Buttons). Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval.
- **Counter-argument:** The hex field applies on every keystroke (`UpdateSourceTrigger=PropertyChanged`), so retyping `#E6E6E6` as `#FF0000` flashes `#FF6E6E` and `#FF006E` through the overlay; LostFocus plus Enter would be calmer, and is a one-line change in the binding if a hauler reports the flicker. No colour wheel: players have to know hex codes; a picker would be a package or a control of our own (backlog if asked). Bounds save at once, the look debounced: two paths in one class. The way out needs the README or knowing where to hover, and only works unlocked. The pencil moved, against the muscle memory of D19; at the minimum width the ship name is cut shorter by one more button.
- **Consequence:** Startup and the settings window can't drift apart, and a move of the overlay no longer races a look change. A broken settings.json is overwritten by the first change in the window as by a move (settings-format.md). T4.2b adds Header, Crates and Buttons with the same building blocks; T4.4 adds hotkeys through the same session.
- **Addendum (T4.2c):** The colour picker is no longer backlog. Our own, no package: the swatch is a toggle that opens a popup with a saturation/brightness pad (`SaturationValuePad`, drawn in `OnRender`, the point-to-value mapping pure) and a hue slider on a spectrum built from `Hsv` (WPF-free, RGB ↔ HSV after the standard formulas). The picker writes `ColourField.Hex`, so picking and typing take one way to the overlay, live while dragging (D30); the alpha stays its own slider; a typed grey keeps the picker's hue, black its saturation, so the hue slider doesn't jump. Esc closes an open picker first, the window only when none is open; the window closes the picker whenever it hides, because a popup is a window of its own and stays when its owner hides, and the moment of hiding is the tractor-beam grab. Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval.
- **Addendum (T4.2b):** Crates, Header and Buttons complete the window, in the order Window, Legend, Legend layout, Crates, Header, Buttons: what a player touches most comes first, and after transparency and legend that is the crate fill (at the lift you want to see the real crates through the planned ones); header and buttons are set once. Each halo says what it surrounds (`HaloEditor.Label`), the crates' one is "Outline around the rack numbers", because there it is a sharp outline and its size a stroke width (D31). The crates' alphas show as percent like every colour's (`ColourField.Percent`); the file keeps 0–255. Counter-argument: `SettingsViewModel` grows to about 20 properties; a view model per section would read better, but has no second user yet (A4). Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval.
- **Counter-argument (T4.2c):** About 200 lines of our own code instead of a package. Every drag step redraws the overlay, twice per mouse move (saturation and brightness are set one after the other); with halos on, the layered window may stutter (D2). A grey colour opens the picker at hue 0 (red). While open, the popup stands above everything, the game included.
- **Addendum (T5.1), exit button:** Exiting needed the taskbar. An exit button (E8BB ChromeClose, tool tip "Exit") now stands at the header's right end, next to the padlock; the gear moves one place left. It is a header button like the others: not focusable, hidden while locked, keeping its space; the padlock stays the only control visible while locked. It calls `Close()` on the overlay, the same way as closing from the taskbar, not `Application.Shutdown()`: what is pending is written by the `Closed` handlers that exist already (`OverlayBoundsSaver`, `SettingsSession.Flush`, `PlanSaver`), so there is no second save path; owned windows close with the overlay, and the last window closing ends the app (`ShutdownMode.OnLastWindowClose`). No confirmation dialog, nothing is lost. Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval. **Counter-argument:** The button sits right next to the padlock, so a click aimed at the lock can end the app without a question; plan, ticks and position are saved, a restart brings them back. At the minimum width the ship name is cut shorter by one more button. The way out of an invisible theme (hover at the header's right end) now finds "Exit" first; the README points to the second button from the right. A power glyph (E7E8) would say "quit the app" more clearly, but reads less familiar at 16 px.

## D35 – Hotkeys recorded in the settings window; refused, not saved, when taken

- **Context:** Hotkeys were set in settings.json only (D17); a typo made the whole file invalid. T4.4 adds a Hotkeys section to the settings window (D34): press the keys instead of typing them, with the same rules and still no defaults.
- **Decision:** **Recording reads key events of our own focused window** (`KeyDown`, `Key.System` → `SystemKey` while Alt is held, `Keyboard.Modifiers`, `KeyInterop.VirtualKeyFromKey`; `RecordedKey`): no hook (D17). A modifier alone waits for the key after it; Esc cancels without closing the window; losing the focus, hiding (auto-lock) or closing the window cancels too. **While recording, our hotkeys are suspended** (`HotkeyService.Suspend`/`Resume`): Windows turns a registered combination into `WM_HOTKEY`, so its keys would never reach the window, and pressing the lock's keys would switch the lock. **Same rules as typed text:** `HotkeyParser.TryCreate` shares its checks and reasons with `TryParse` (Ctrl, Alt or Win required, F12 reserved, only keys from the table); a refused combination shows its reason at the button and recording goes on. **A combination that is taken is refused and not saved:** the other action's combination by name ("already used for Lock / unlock", a pure check in `HotkeysViewModel`), another program's by `RegisterHotKey` failing (`HotkeyService.TrySet` puts the old hotkey back). Only a registered hotkey is saved (`SettingsSession.ChangeHotkey`, debounced like the look), as canonical text; **Clear writes `null`**, so the entry stays visible, and `null` and missing read the same. The hint says Ctrl, Alt or Win is required and that Win combinations may be taken by Windows itself. No "Reset to defaults" in this section: the default is no hotkey (D17), which is Clear. `HotkeyEditor` and `HotkeysViewModel` are WPF-free; the section has its own data context, `SettingsViewModel` stays the look. Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval.
- **Counter-argument:** T3.7 saved a taken combination and reported it in the overlay; refusing it means a combination another program frees later has to be set again. A combination another program has registered probably never reaches the window, so recording it does nothing and only the hint explains why; the "in use by another program" path is mainly for programs that take it between pressing and registering. Win combinations are often taken by the shell. While recording, our hotkeys don't work. Two view model classes for two actions; one would be shorter, but the duplicate check needs both. Alt+Space may still open the window's system menu.
- **Consequence:** settings.json need not be edited for anything in the settings window any more. A hotkey invalid in the file shows in the overlay's notice line and at its button; Clear or a new combination removes it. The startup notice for a taken combination points at the settings window.

## D36 – Tool windows in WPF's Fluent theme, per window, with a fixed teal accent

- **Context:** Settings window, mission editor and colour picker had the system look (D34) and glared over the dark game. Wanted: three fixed modes light, dark and system (follows Windows live); the overlay keeps `appearance` (D34). Two ways: **F**, WPF's Fluent theme through `Window.ThemeMode` (.NET 9+, experimental, `WPF0001`), or **E**, own templates for about 14 controls with a palette record, a `WM_SETTINGCHANGE` watcher and the DWM title bar. A spike (T4.7a) decided between them against seven gates; it opens a Debug-only sample window (`--theme-sample`) next to the overlay, not alone, because gates 1 and 7 judge the two side by side.
- **Spike T4.7a, per gate:** (1) The overlay stays pixel-identical (`RenderTargetBitmap`, 0 px), `Application.ThemeMode` stays `None`, no dictionary is merged into the application. (2) Fluent controls are readable in light and dark, with three faults: the hue slider lost its spectrum (Fluent's slider doesn't draw `Background`), a disabled slider or text box looked enabled, and the picker popup stayed white (`SystemColors`); all three are fixed in the sample (below). Tool tip, context menu and the DataGrid edit cell: Martin's check in the game. (3) The title bar follows the mode; following a Windows switch live: Martin's check. (4) The settings window shows a mixed look: own styles without `BasedOn` fall back to Aero2. (5) The teal accent works through the seven `SystemColors.AccentColor*Key` colours, the only accent source Fluent reads (DynamicResources in its `Light.xaml`/`Dark.xaml`); `WPF0001` is suppressed by `#pragma` in `ToolWindowThemer` only, not with `NoWarn`. (6) Markings follow the mode through Fluent's `SystemFillColorCritical/Caution(+Background)Brush`. (7) Martin's look next to the overlay: F.
- **Decision:** **Way F with a fixed teal accent.** `ToolWindowThemer.Apply(window, ToolWindowTheme)` sets `ThemeMode` on each tool window, never on the application, and the accent into the window's resources; `ToolWindowTheme` (System, Light, Dark; `default` = System) is WPF-free. Fluent follows Windows in `System` mode by itself and switches to its own high-contrast dictionary, so there is no watcher, no DWM call and no own template. **Pattern for own styles**, found in the sample: `Apply` runs before `InitializeComponent`, and sets `ThemeMode` before it touches `Resources`, because the setter loads Fluent at once only while the window has no resources yet and otherwise waits for the window handle, after the XAML. Own styles are `BasedOn` Fluent's and stand **one level below the window** (the root panel's resources): a style keyed `{x:Type Slider}` that looks up `{x:Type Slider}` skips its own dictionary, the merged Fluent one included, and ends up with no base at all. Rows base on Fluent's keyed `DefaultDataGridRowStyle`; there is no implicit one. A base taken at load still switches between light and dark, because Fluent swaps its dictionary in place and its templates use DynamicResources. Fixes: `HueSliderStyle` (App.xaml, keyed) draws the spectrum as the track with Fluent's `SliderThumbStyle`; disabled sliders and text boxes fade to opacity 0.4 like the overlay's icon buttons; the popup uses `FlyoutBackground`/`FlyoutBorderBrush`. Fluent's row and cell templates paint a fixed transparent background, so an invalid row can't be tinted any more; it gets a 1 px frame in `SystemFillColorCriticalBrush` instead (the row template binds `BorderBrush`), chosen over red text. Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval.
- **Counter-argument:** `ThemeMode` is experimental and may change with .NET 11. We lean on Fluent's internal keys (`DefaultDataGridRowStyle`, `SliderThumbStyle`, `SliderButtonStyle`, `FlyoutBackground`, `SystemFillColor*`), which are no public API and can be renamed without notice; a missing key shows only in the look, never as a build error. The rule "own styles one level below the window" is not obvious: a style put into `Window.Resources` silently falls back to Aero2. The look is Windows 11, not sci-fi; Fluent's controls are larger, so the settings window grows. The tinted invalid row is gone. File dialogs and message boxes stay in the system look.
- **Consequence:** T4.7b adds the setting and wires settings window and editor after this pattern: `Apply` in their constructors before `InitializeComponent`, own styles moved from `Window.Resources` to the root panel with `BasedOn` (the editor's `RowStyle` on `DefaultDataGridRowStyle`, the invalid row framed), `HueSliderStyle`, Fluent keys for popup and markings, and `InvalidRowBrush`, `EditorProblemBrush` and `EditorWarningBrush` removed from App.xaml. D34's "system look" for the tool windows is replaced; its reason holds, because the tool windows' colours stay fixed and can't be themed away. **Done in T4.7b:** `"toolWindowTheme"` (`system`, `light`, `dark`) at the top level of settings.json, so a section's reset never switches it; missing and `null` follow Windows without a notice, an unknown name with one, like `legend.position`. The overlay owns both tool windows, so the settings window's choice goes through it: the session saves debounced, the overlay calls `Apply` on the open windows, new ones take the mode in their constructor. Counter-argument: a third tool window must be added there by hand; iterating `Application.Current.Windows` would find it, but would tie the session to a running application its tests don't have. No `Resolve` (system plus the Windows value to light or dark): only way E's watcher needed it (back with the addendum below). The sample's mode box now switches only the sample, or it would fight the setting. Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval.
- **Addendum T4.7b, grey text in dark mode:** Fluent's dark text is pure white (`TextFillColorPrimary`) and glared next to the game. Fluent builds 55 brushes from that colour by `StaticResource` (`WindowForeground`, `ButtonForeground`, `TextControlForeground`…), so the colour can't be overridden, only the brushes. `ToolWindowThemer` loads Fluent's `Dark.xaml`, takes every brush of that colour except the focus ring and selected text on the accent, and merges one grey set (`#D0D0D0`, chosen by eye from `#E6E6E6`, `#D0D0D0`, `#BEBEBE`) after Fluent's dictionary, only while the window shows dark and not in high contrast. For `System` that needs the Windows mode after all: `ToolWindowThemes.Resolve` reads `AppsUseLightTheme` like Fluent does, and `SystemEvents.UserPreferenceChanged` reapplies it to the open windows. **Counter-argument:** the "no watcher" of the decision above is gone, and two more internal Fluent names (`TextFillColorPrimary`, the path of `Dark.xaml`) can break silently. Greying only for a fixed "Dark" was cheaper, but would make "Follow Windows" with dark Windows look different from "Dark". Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval.

## D37 – Import before release

- **Context:** TASKS.md had the release as phase 5 and the ContractTracker V2 import as phase 6, "because reach comes first". The tool isn't public yet, so nobody waits for a download. The haulers it is for mostly run ContractTracker alongside the game and don't write mission files; for them typing every contract into the editor is the hurdle, and a first release would be judged by it.
- **Decision:** The import becomes **phase 5**, the release **phase 6**; the extension stays phase 7. Phase 5: exit button and notices fixed at the bottom left (T5.1), schema (T5.2), read-only reader with a schema fingerprint (T5.3), sync into the existing plan (T5.4), a ContractTracker section in the settings window (T5.5). Phase 6 starts with backing up settings.json before its first overwrite (T6.1, a release precondition), followed by the former T5.1–T5.3 unchanged as T6.2–T6.4. Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval.
- **Counter-argument:** A release without an outside dependency would be simpler: no closed-source schema to follow, no "not supported" path, and early feedback from haulers without ContractTracker. The release moves back by a whole phase, and that phase holds the first Core change since T3.15 (sync into the plan).
- **Consequence:** The late phases are renumbered a second time; references in TASKS.md and D23 follow. ContractTracker is closed source, so any update can change its schema without notice: the reader must check a fingerprint (the tables and columns it reads) before reading, and refuse with a notice pointing to the editor and its Import… button instead of reading wrongly. Whether we may read and document the database is asked of its author (T5.2). (revised by D39: ContractTracker prefills the editor, no sync)

## D38 – ContractTracker database: what we read and how it behaves

- **Context:** Before the reader (T5.3) and the sync (T5.4), T5.2 looked at the database of ContractTracker V2 (closed source) with the `sqlite3` CLI, on copies only: the schema first, then a lifecycle protocol where Martin used ContractTracker 2.2.0.0 and every step was read as a consistent read-only snapshot (`.backup`) plus a count of the commit frames in the write-ahead log. Table and column names, types and the mapping are in [contracttracker-schema.md](contracttracker-schema.md); this entry names concepts only, so it stays valid when an update renames something, and holds no copy of the schema. Licence question asked of the author in the Nexus Discord on 2026-10-20 09:26; no answer yet. If the answer is no, the schema file goes, this entry stays.
- **Decision:** The database is found at **one fixed path**, `%APPDATA%\SCContractTracker\contracts.db`. The **fingerprint** is the list of (table, column, declared type) the reader reads, in the schema file: each one must exist with its type, anything else (more columns, more tables) is ignored. There is **no version signal** to check instead: `user_version` is 0 and there is no migrations table. Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval.
- **Findings:**

| Question | Finding | Consequence for T5.3/T5.4 |
|---|---|---|
| Where is the database, can it be configured? | `%APPDATA%\SCContractTracker\contracts.db`, next to ContractTracker's own settings file, which holds no database path. Nothing under `%LOCALAPPDATA%`. The program itself can live anywhere (Martin: `C:\Tools\…`). | Autodetection is one path (T5.5); the manual path is for the exception. |
| Pragmas | `user_version` 0, `journal_mode` WAL, `schema_version` 13 (unchanged by the whole protocol), UTF-8, 4096-byte pages. | No version to compare; the fingerprint is the only guard. Reading must cope with WAL. |
| Which data is there? | A **contract row is one commodity**, not one in-game contract: commodity name, pickup as text, reward, status, capture time (UTC without a marker), the raw OCR text. Destinations with SCU are **child rows** of it, with a completed flag. A session table and a location mapping table (OCR text → correct name) stand beside them. | Commodity and SCU per destination come straight from rows; no string parsing for the core data. |
| Max crate size? | **Not in a column.** It shows up only in the contract's prose inside the raw OCR text ("none of the containers are bigger than 4 SCU"), if at all. | D23's "ContractTracker stores the same two" does not hold: the reader needs a default or the player's entry in the editor. Open: default value, and whether parsing the prose is worth it. |
| Several pickups / destinations? | Several **pickups** become one text with " / " between them, mixed with OCR noise. Several **destinations** become child rows, but one in-game contract with one commodity and three destinations came out as two contract rows (2 + 1 destinations) with the same OCR text and the same capture time. | Destinations are usable; the pickup text is not. One in-game contract is not one row; grouping rows needs the OCR text or the capture time, so it is avoided: the plan groups by destination anyway. |
| Reward? | Column exists, 0 in every row observed (OCR missed it). | Not read; the plan has no reward. |
| Completed, deleted, reset: flag or delete? | There is **no separate completion**: a contract leaves only by deleting it in the app (× / "Delete Contract") or by reset (F5, "clear all contracts from this session"); Martin knows no other way in the 2.2 interface. Both **delete** rows (child rows cascade): one transaction per deleted contract, **one transaction for all** on reset. Status stayed "Active" and the completed flag 0 throughout; nothing completes a single destination. | "Row gone = fulfilled" (T5.4) is all the database says; deleted and delivered can't be told apart. Reset is visible as "all gone in one read"; with one contract left, reset and delivering it look the same. Open: whether status or completed flag are ever set. |
| Edit (OCR correction) | "Edit Pickup Location" / "Edit Destination" open the mapping tab; saving **inserts one mapping row and changes no contract row**: the destination stays as captured, also on screen. Whether a later capture applies the mapping is open (not observable in the protocol). | An edit is never "contract gone + new contract"; positions and ticks are safe. A mapping write is watcher noise without a contract change, so sync must **diff, not react**. Applying the mappings ourselves is an option for T5.3 (it would extend the fingerprint). |
| Ids after a reset? | Ids are AUTOINCREMENT and **never reused**: after deletes and a reset the next contract continued the sequence. | The ids are a stable identity as long as the file lives; a recreated database (reinstall) restarts them and reads like a reset. |
| Same contract twice (A)? | Capturing the same contract again added a **second row**, with a slightly different pickup text and OCR text. Retry (F4, "Retry (recapture)" in ContractTracker's hotkey list) adds another row too and replaces nothing, despite its name. A first repeated capture wrote nothing; whether that was duplicate detection or a missed key is open. | The database can't tell a double capture from two equal contracts: sync must count (a multiset), not treat equal content as one. The pickup can't be part of a content key: the same contract gave three different pickup texts. T5.3's key "destination + commodity + SCU + pickup" has to be revised there; the ids are the candidate. |
| Quality of a capture | The same contract, captured twice, gave three destinations once and **one** the next time; destinations carry OCR tails ("… A new haul just popped up") (corrected in the addendum: these tails are the contract's own prose). | The import is incomplete and noisy in a way that changes per capture. Open for T5.3: how the player sees and corrects that (editor review, notice), instead of the sync passing it on silently. |
| Files while running and after exit | ContractTracker creates the WAL and shared-memory files at start, but **not for good**: once during the protocol it checkpointed, deleted both and created them anew a minute later (trigger unknown). On exit it checkpoints into the database and deletes both. Starting and exiting without a capture leaves the database untouched. | The watcher must watch the database **and** the WAL including their creation and deletion; a checkpoint changes the database file without new content (more noise, diff handles it). Between checkpoints the database file does not change at all. |
| Write pattern of a capture | **One transaction per capture**, written within about 40 ms of the capture time; no follow-up writes, no rollback journal. | One capture is one watcher event; the 500 ms debounce of T5.4 is enough. |
| Our own read-only access | A read-only connection to a WAL database while ContractTracker is closed **leaves an empty WAL and a 32 KB shared-memory file behind**: it may not delete them. | `Mode=ReadOnly` does not mean "touches no file". Reading while ContractTracker is closed leaves files in its folder and triggers our own watcher; T5.3/T5.4 must allow for that (ignore our own events, or read only while the WAL exists). |

- **Counter-argument:** The fingerprint checks names and types, not meaning: an update that reuses a column with the same name for something else passes it and is read wrongly. Ignoring extra columns lets harmless updates through but also those that move data into a new column. Everything above is one session with one version and Martin's captures; deleting rows instead of flagging them can change with any update without the schema changing. The ids as identity tie the sync to one database file.
- **Consequence:** T5.3 reads only the fingerprint columns and has to decide its key (ids instead of the pickup), the max crate size and how a noisy import reaches the player; the fixture `tests/CargoPlan.Import.Tests/Fixtures/ct_schema.sql` holds just those columns. T5.4 diffs every read against the plan, counts equal content, treats "all gone in one read" as reset, and watches the database and the WAL. A changed ContractTracker means a new protocol run; the schema file is where names change.
- **Addendum (same session, re-read of the snapshots):** The raw OCR text of all seven contract rows (six captures) was read in full; it never changes after the capture.
  - **Destinations: the gaps come from ContractTracker's parsing, not from the capture.** The Stims capture with one destination holds all three "Deliver … to …" lines and the full drop-off list; two of the lines carry OCR errors ("Dellver O/3", "O0/5", "L5" read as "LS"). The 2 + 1 split holds all three as well. Destination tails are the contract's own prose read out of line order; in the Silicon capture "A new haul just popped up" is the contract's opening sentence, not text from the list beside it.
  - **Text from the contract list beside it** (truncated lines such as "· Stims (3 SCU)", "Lagrange point. A new haul jus") is in 3 of 6 captures, not all; in these rows it never reached the destinations.
  - **Max crate size** is in 2 of 3 contracts (4 of 6 captures), in two wordings: "Max size will be 1 SCU." and "Don't worry, none of the containers are bigger than 4 SCU." (with a line break before "worry"). Observed in English only; a German game client is not covered by this protocol. The Stims contract shows none in either capture; whether it has one is not observable.
  - **The two rows of one capture have byte-identical raw text** (1,300 bytes) and pickup texts that differ. The raw text works as a key for grouping the rows of one capture, not one in-game contract: the second Stims capture has a different raw text.
  - **Consequence:** T5.3 parses the raw text and reads the child rows only as a fallback; the parser has to tolerate OCR errors in the "Deliver" lines and takes its fixtures from these raw texts (anonymised, no contract date). The parser reads only between "DETAILS" and "ACCEPT OFFER" and ignores everything outside: in every capture with list fragments (· Stims (3 SCU), truncated lines) they sit in the header before "DETAILS", and only digit noise follows "ACCEPT OFFER"; the fixtures include captures with such fragments. No README note on the capture region, because the parser handles list text, not because it is rare.
- **Addendum (T7.4d):** `Contracts.PickupLocation` is read now (D68) and joins the fingerprint. "The pickup text is not usable" holds for a key, not for the player: it is split at "/" and otherwise shown as captured, and it stays out of the duplicate rule. Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval.
- **Addendum (B6, D69):** "Shown as captured" ended at contract 38, whose pickup field held the next list line ("Everus Harbor. - Freight elevator at Green Glade Station …"). Each place is now cut at a list line's dash and at the DROP OFF/PICK UP LOCATIONS headings and goes through the destination's normalisation (sentence end, " at …", " above …", LS → L5); spelling noise in the name stays. Plans saved before are cleaned once on loading. Still no part of the duplicate rule. Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval.

## D39 – ContractTracker prefills the editor; we parse the raw text

- **Context:** D37 planned a sync from ContractTracker into the plan (T5.4). The D38 addendum revised the premise of D37: a capture is incomplete in ContractTracker's rows, but its raw OCR text holds every destination; ContractTracker's parsing loses them.
- **Decision:** ContractTracker becomes a **prefill for the editor, not a sync source**: the reader yields import candidates the player reviews before they reach the plan. We **parse the raw text ourselves**; ContractTracker's child rows are the fallback. A candidate is keyed by its **ContractTracker ids**; new ones are read above a **watermark** (the highest reviewed id, accepted or discarded) that belongs to the plan; no content key. Reason from the game: a silent sync with missing destinations shows up at the freight elevator, and a plan you distrust is worse than none. **Revision of D37, ratified by approval.**
- **Counter-argument:** One more click and one review per contract; D37's idea of contracts showing up in the overlay without a step in between is dead. That is the deliberate price. Parsing ourselves means **we** follow layout changes in the game, not ContractTracker.
- **Consequence:** T5.3 is reader plus raw-text parser and yields candidates; T5.4 is review in the editor plus incremental placement, without reset detection, "gone = fulfilled", multiset and content debounce; T5.5 switches a notice, not a sync. D38's consequences for T5.4 (multiset, reset detection) are superseded; "diff, don't react" stays and now means: a file event yields a notice only if rows above the watermark exist.
- **Addendum (same day, nothing built yet): review moves from the editor to the legend.** Revision of D39's review location, ratified by approval.
  - **Context:** In the game a hauler presses F3 at the terminal and looks at the overlay; the editor is opened only when something is wrong. A mandatory review in the editor would cost a window and a click per contract at the terminal, too cumbersome to be used.
  - **Decision:** Candidates go **straight into the plan**; the watermark rises when a candidate is applied. Whatever is uncertain is **marked in the legend** at its stop: crate size assumed ("4 SCU?"), destinations incomplete ("⚠ 1 of 3 destinations"), fallback to ContractTracker's child rows. **Every capture becomes a mission of its own, nothing is replaced:** a capture with the same commodity and overlapping destinations as a mission without ticks is marked "⚠ duplicate?", with no notice; the marking disappears with the first tick on either. The player decides at the elevator: two real contracts → ignore the marking; a retry after an OCR error → delete the wrong mission in the editor. Reason from the game: the plan must rather know too many crates than too few. A crate too many is an empty cell at the elevator, one glance; a crate too few has no place, and the hauler improvises exactly where the tool should take the load off. Replacing is silent data loss, adding is visible and reversible. **Deleted in ContractTracker:** all crates of the mission ticked → the mission leaves the plan; otherwise a notice, the plan stays.
  - **Unchanged:** the core of D39: our own raw-text parser with the child rows as fallback, candidates keyed by ContractTracker ids above a watermark that belongs to the plan, no content identity across captures; the duplicate check only marks, it never merges.
  - **Counter-argument:** A phantom destination sits in the plan until the player removes it. A retry leaves a duplicate that takes rack space until it is deleted: in a full 64 SCU Hull A that can be the rack the next contract needs. Both are the price of zero clicks, made visible by the markings.
  - **Consequence:** T5.4 becomes auto-apply with markings plus incremental placement; applied missions keep their ContractTracker ids in the plan, needed for the deletion notice; removing a mission (deleted in ContractTracker, deleted in the editor) moves no other crate. "Sync from ContractTracker" in the editor stays as the manual way. T5.5 switches auto-sync again. T5.3 is unchanged; its hints feed the legend instead of a review. Superseded in D39: review before the plan, the watermark as "highest reviewed id" (now: highest applied), the one-click-per-contract counter-argument. "Gone = fulfilled" returns only in the narrow form above (deleted and fully ticked).

- **Addendum (T5.4 planning): duplicate rule, markings, first sync.** Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval.
  - **Duplicate = subset, not overlap.** A mission is marked "⚠ duplicate?" if the (destination, commodity) pairs it delivers are all delivered by another mission, or the other way round; overlapping alone is not enough. Evidence: contracts 36 and 37/38 share two destinations and are both real. For one commodity this is "same commodity, destinations of one a subset of the other's". Of the two, the later is marked. Neither may have a ticked crate. The marking is **derived**, not stored: it goes with the first tick on either and comes back with ticking back, and the legend's reserve (D16) can count it, so a tick moves no line.
  - **No marking for the child-row fallback.** Missing destinations are what matters at the elevator; "⚠ n of m destinations" shows them where the contract has a drop-off list.
  - **First sync takes everything in ContractTracker** (watermark 0), also for a plan migrated from schema 2: ContractTracker deletes only by × or F5, so its list is the player's open contracts.
  - **Counter-argument:** A player who never tidies ContractTracker gets every old contract into the plan at the first start, many of them "doesn't fit". Subset misses a retry that read a different, wrong destination (OCR): no marking, two missions.
  - **Addendum (T5.3b): the reward decides too.** Context: single-destination contracts to the same place with the same commodity are everyday Covalex work (Waste to Everus Harbor, ids 47 and 48); for them the subset rule is always true, and every second contract was marked. Decision: a duplicate needs the subset **and** the same reward, or at least one reward unknown (typed mission, capture without a readable "Reward" line, plan from before T5.3b). The parser reads the reward from the start of the raw text, only if its line is a number and nothing else; `ContractSource` carries it, the plan file keeps it (optional field, schema stays 3). Counter-argument: a retry whose reward OCR misread as another number is no longer marked; reading only a line that is wholly a number keeps that rare ("5O,250" is unknown, not 5). Two real contracts with the same destinations and the same reward are still marked. Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval.

## D40 – One normalisation, in the parser

- **Context:** T5.4 marks a capture "⚠ duplicate?" when it has the same commodity and overlapping destinations as a mission without ticks. That only works if names from different captures of one contract come out equal. The raw texts of T5.2 show why they don't by themselves: destination lines end in " at"/" above" or are cut off mid-name ("to Thundering", "to Thundering Express"), "LS" stands for "L5", and ContractTracker's destination rows carry tails of prose. Normalising in the parser and normalising in the comparison are the two places it could go.
- **Decision:** **The parser normalises, the comparison doesn't.** One class turns OCR text into names, called by the raw-text parser and by the child-row fallback alike: whitespace collapsed, "LS" → "L5", the destination cut to the **station name** before " at …"/" above …"/the end of the sentence ("Everus Harbor above Hurston" → "Everus Harbor"). A destination line without such an end may be cut off; it is completed from a longer name in the same text that starts with it (drop-off list, prose, the other deliveries). T5.4 compares with the comparer destinations use everywhere (ordinal, ignoring case). The anchors of the contract text ("DETAILS", "ACCEPT OFFER") are matched tolerantly (O/0, I/L/1 alike) and are optional: a capture from the "Accepted" tab, not observed, has no "ACCEPT OFFER". Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval.
- **Counter-argument:** Normalising is lossy: the raw form is gone, and a wrong correction can't be seen in the plan (the raw text stays in ContractTracker and comes out with `contracts --raw`). Missions typed in the editor don't pass through the parser, so a duplicate between a capture and a typed mission is found only if the names happen to match. Cutting at " at " breaks a station whose name contains " at ". When no full name is in the text, a cut-off destination stays a fragment ("Thundering Express"), and a later complete capture of the same station does not match it.
- **Consequence:** The normalised name is not only compared, it is the stop in the plan and the label in the overlay; normalising only in the comparison would have put "Thundering" and "Thundering Express" into two racks. Candidates carry normalised names; T5.4 needs no key of its own. A new OCR error class is fixed in one place and covered by a fixture from a bug report (`CargoPlan.Cli contracts <db> --raw`).
- **Addendum (T5.3, same day): completion by character prefix, from the drop-off list only.** Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval.
  - **Context:** A capture from the Accepted tab (ids 37/38) broke a delivery line mid-word: "to Thunde", with "oss Station" two lines further down, and cut "to High Course" off. Completion by word prefix missed both. The Accepted view ends in TRACK/ABANDON, not ACCEPT OFFER; DETAILS is in both views (contracttracker-schema.md, "Raw text layouts").
  - **Decision:** A destination without a name end is completed in two steps. First from the **drop-off list** only, by **character prefix**, and only if **exactly one** entry starts with it. Then a fragment left over takes the one longer destination of the same capture that starts with it (ids 28/29 have no list). Otherwise the fragment stays. The pick-up list and "freight elevator at …" in the prose no longer complete anything.
  - **Counter-argument:** A character prefix matches more than a word prefix: a fragment of one letter takes the only list entry starting with it, right or wrong. Two rules instead of one. Without a drop-off list, a mid-word fragment ("Thunde") stays unless another delivery of the capture completes it.
  - **Consequence:** "Thunde" → "Thundering Express Station", "High Course" → "High Course Station"; two list entries with the same start keep the fragment, and the overlay shows the fragment as the destination. A pickup that shares a destination's prefix can't be taken for it.
- **Addendum (T5.3b): hardened against line breaks and missing words.** Precision in the planning phase, ratified by approval.
